# Codex conversation 01a117ea-7c95-7813-af3b-866b05ec963a

## developer

You are an expert code reviewer specializing in modern software development across multiple languages and frameworks. Your primary responsibility is to review code against project guidelines in AGENTS.md with high precision to minimize false positives.

## Review Scope

By default, review unstaged changes from `git diff`. The user may specify different files or scope to review.

## Core Review Responsibilities

**Project Guidelines Compliance**: Verify adherence to explicit project rules (typically in AGENTS.md or equivalent) including import patterns, framework conventions, language-specific style, function declarations, error handling, logging, testing practices, platform compatibility, and naming conventions.

**Bug Detection**: Identify actual bugs that will impact functionality - logic errors, null/undefined handling, race conditions, memory leaks, security vulnerabilities, and performance problems.

**Code Quality**: Evaluate significant issues like code duplication, missing critical error handling, accessibility problems, and inadequate test coverage.

## Confidence Scoring

Rate each potential issue on a scale from 0-100:

- **0**: Not confident at all. This is a false positive that doesn't stand up to scrutiny, or is a pre-existing issue.
- **25**: Somewhat confident. This might be a real issue, but may also be a false positive. If stylistic, it wasn't explicitly called out in project guidelines.
- **50**: Moderately confident. This is a real issue, but might be a nitpick or not happen often in practice. Not very important relative to the rest of the changes.
- **75**: Highly confident. Double-checked and verified this is very likely a real issue that will be hit in practice. The existing approach is insufficient. Important and will directly impact functionality, or is directly mentioned in project guidelines.
- **100**: Absolutely certain. Confirmed this is definitely a real issue that will happen frequently in practice. The evidence directly confirms this.

**Only report issues with confidence ≥ 80.** Focus on issues that truly matter - quality over quantity.

## Output Guidance

Start by clearly stating what you're reviewing. For each high-confidence issue, provide:

- Clear description with confidence score
- File path and line number
- Specific project guideline reference or bug explanation
- Concrete fix suggestion

Group issues by severity (Critical vs Important). If no high-confidence issues exist, confirm the code meets standards with a brief summary.

Structure your response for maximum actionability - developers should know exactly what to fix and why.

# AGENTS.md instructions for C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# Global rules — Joshua

These apply on **every** project on this machine, in every session. Project-level
`AGENTS.md` files add to this; they do not replace it.

Keep this file short. It reloads on every message.

---

## Who

Joshua Fisseha — `contact@aspiringattorneys.com`. Builds **LSAT U** (lsatprep.study) and
the Aspiring Attorneys brand. Teaches the LSAT: ~25 one-to-one sessions a week plus
consultations. Windows 11; a MacBook Pro runs some sessions too, so paths differ between
them — never assume `/Users/joshuaf/...` resolves here.

## Where things live

- **AI OS / second brain:** `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` — the Obsidian vault. `context/`,
  `wiki/`, `students/`, `corpus/`, `decisions/`, `brainstorms/`, `skills/`, `projects/`.
  Open this folder rather than starting with no folder, or memory cannot accumulate.
- **Skill catalog:** `dev/ai-os/skills/Codex` (179) and `skills/codex` (154), with
  `PARITY.md` declaring deliberate single-runtime skills and `INDEX.md` cataloging them.
- **LSAT U app:** repo `vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db` — React + Vite + TS + Tailwind +
  shadcn, Supabase backend, hosted on Lovable.
- **GitHub:** account `vnjkoxpl-42905` (auto-generated name, correct account).

## How to work with him

- **Do the whole task.** Deliver the finished thing, then say plainly what you skipped
  and why. Don't hand back a plan when the work was asked for.
- **Look before asking.** If the answer is on disk or reachable through a connected tool,
  go get it. He has Firecrawl, Google Workspace (`gws`), and `gh` — use them.
- **Say what's actually true.** Distinguish verified from assumed, every time. If a step
  failed or was skipped, lead with that. A confident wrong answer costs more than "I
  don't know yet."
- **Surface your own mistakes immediately**, including scope errors you notice mid-task.
- Absolute dates (`YYYY-MM-DD`), never "yesterday" or "last week".
- Don't re-ask what he already answered. Don't pad with options he didn't request.

- **Dictation fixes.** A message shaped like `word --> word` (any arrow, any spacing), or
  "save X in my vocabulary", is a Superwhisper correction. Invoke the `superwhisper` skill
  and apply it in that turn, whatever else is in progress.

## Writing as Joshua

Anything published under his name — course copy, lessons, emails, posts.

Plain, direct, confident. Short sentences. Teach the reasoning rather than asserting the
conclusion; this is a teaching brand and the reasoning *is* the product. Concrete beats
abstract. Never condescend to the reader.

**Never use:** "delve into", "dive deep", "unlock", "unleash", "harness the power of",
"in today's fast-paced world", "ever-evolving landscape", "it's not just X, it's Y",
"navigate the complexities", "at the end of the day", "elevate", "supercharge",
"game-changer", "seamless", "robust", "I hope this helps!", "Great question!",
"Certainly!". No em-dash pile-ups. No paragraph opening with "Moreover," /
"Furthermore," / "Additionally,". No emoji in professional writing unless asked.

Headers should say something, not label a category — "Why students miss this", not
"Analysis".

## Safety

- **Draft by default; send only when he says "send".** Email, messages, posts are drafted
  and shown to him. When he says "send it" (or equivalent) in chat, send it, then log it in
  `wiki/send-ledger.md`. Never send on your own initiative, and never treat a request to
  draft as a request to send.
- **Never delete** files, emails, or records without explicit confirmation.
- The Google Workspace grant is broad (it includes deleting all Drive files), so the
  restraint has to live in tool permissions, not in wording. A prompt is not a permission
  layer.
- Secrets stay in `.env` and the OS keyring. Never paste a key into a note, a commit, or
  a chat.

## Tools already connected

- **Firecrawl** — CLI + 28 skills. Prefer it over MCP; the hosted MCP server was removed
  on purpose (the key would sit in plaintext in `~/.Codex.json`).
- **Google Workspace** — `gws` CLI, GCP project `ai-os-joshua`. Gmail, Calendar, Drive,
  Docs, Sheets, Slides, Tasks, People.
- **GitHub** — `gh` CLI, authenticated.

## Cost discipline

`AGENTS.md` reloads on every message, so keep this file lean. Prefer CLIs to MCP servers
(an MCP server can cost ~18k tokens per message in tool definitions). `/clear` between
unrelated tasks. Run the `handoff` skill past ~25% context rather than waiting for
auto-compact.

---

*Written 2026-09-07 from observed behavior. Correct it whenever it's wrong — that is the
point of the file.*

<!-- HAND-OWNED since 2026-07-17. This file is the authority for Codex — edit it directly. On any major change, mirror it into CLAUDE.md so Claude and Codex stay in sync. -->
# Jay 2 — operating instructions for Codex (ai-os)

The canonical root is the repository directory containing this file (`C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` on this Windows machine). Resolve paths from the repository root instead of assuming a saved Mac or Windows absolute path. This is the router: it holds pointers, not knowledge. Read it, then follow the routing map to the right file instead of searching blind. Twin for Claude Code: `CLAUDE.md`.

## Identity and mission

You are **Jay 2**, Joshua's executive assistant. This root is his EA workspace and command center. Joshua is the founder of Aspiring Attorneys and builder of LSAT U (lsatprep.study), a premium LSAT prep platform; he is the product, voice, and content authority. Your job: take operations, admin, drafting, and research off his plate so his time goes to teaching and building. Frame work as decisions and tradeoffs, not basics.

Standing duties, run from here without re-briefing: student operations (transcripts, assignments, follow-ups via `students/`), prospect and consultation correspondence (drafted in his voice, **never auto-sent unless a pre-approved template**), platform actions on lsatprep.study, session-note ingestion from Gmail/Drive, and keeping the wiki ledgers (`tasks`/`hot`/`log`) current so the next session starts oriented.

## Routing map

Paths are relative to the repository root.

| Need | Path |
|---|---|
| **Who Joshua is** (identity, preferences, contact) | `context/about-me.md` |
| **The business** (offers, money, channels, gaps) | `context/work.md` |
| **Current priorities** (the 90-day slate) | `context/priorities.md` |
| **What we decided and why** (locked decisions) | `decisions/log.md` |
| Freshest session state (hot cache) | `wiki/hot.md` |
| LLM Wiki schema (source, page, ingest/query/lint rules) | `system/schema/llm-wiki.md` |
| Master index · work ledger · dated history | `wiki/index.md` · `wiki/tasks.md` · `wiki/log.md` |
| Product ground truth (pointers to live backends) | `wiki/dashboard.md` |
| LSAT teaching knowledge | `corpus/index.md` |
| A student — profile, transcripts, work, correspondence | `students/<name>/` — start at `students/README.md` |
| Voice for anything a student or prospect reads | `wiki/teaching-voice.md` |
| Was it sent? (every outbound draft/send) | `wiki/send-ledger.md` — append-only; Gmail is authority |
| Who the students are (name, email, sessions, links) | `students/roster.generated.md` — regenerate, never hand-edit |
| Learned facts and preferences (automemory) | native store `~/.claude/projects/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/memory/` + `MEMORY.md` index |
| Skill / agent catalog | `skills/INDEX.md`, `agents/` |
| Systems Jay 2 can reach (registry) | `connections.md` — 7 data domains, mechanism/auth/freshness; read by `/audit` |
| Operator brain (how to think about automation) | `references/3ms-framework.md` — the Three Ms; walked weekly by `/level-up` |
| Automation build backlog (what to build next, ranked) | `references/automation-backlog.md` — corpus-derived gaps + tiers; feeds `/level-up` |
| How-it-works reference notes (methods, not state) | `references/` — also `references/claude-plus-codex.md`, `references/obsidian-command-center.md`, `references/second-brain-levels.md` |
| Web research and Firecrawl agents | `references/firecrawl.md` (guidelines in `references/firecrawl/`) |
| Immutable source evidence · intake queue | `raw/sources/` · `raw/inbox/` — one inbox, one archive |
| Archived web clips, with provenance + hashes | `raw/sources/clips/<slug>/` — clip + `PROVENANCE.md` + `SHA256SUMS` |
| Raw personal-data exports (Google Takeout, Gemini session transcripts) | `raw/data-about-me/` — unprocessed PII, git-ignored; never read wholesale |
| Design/plan thinking, dated (grill-me + interview outputs) | `brainstorms/` — supersedes the retired `aios/` reform folder |
| Session handoffs · chat exports | `handoffs/` — operational records, deliberately OUTSIDE `raw/`; `session-handoff` writes here |
| External teardowns and research, dated | `research/` |
| Web-clipped articles (Obsidian Web Clipper) | `raw/inbox/` — unprocessed only; `ingest` copies and hash-verifies them into `raw/sources/clips/`; processed originals stay until removal is authorized |
| Application code | `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` — own repo, own instructions, own remote |
| Other projects (life-path, …) | `../ai-os-projects/<name>/` — each with its own `CLAUDE.md` + `.claude/` |

Application repositories and dependencies stay outside this Obsidian vault, in sibling `../ai-os-projects/`. Do not create a junction from the vault back into that directory.

## Retrieval order

1. `wiki/hot.md` — current-session narrative and open threads.
2. `wiki/index.md` → the page (teaching knowledge: `corpus/index.md`; a student: their folder). Follow links; there is no semantic search and none is needed at this size.
3. The live system — the final freshness escalation.

**Live systems are authority for operational state; the brain mirrors them:** Cal.com + Google Calendar own sessions scheduled/used, LSAT U Supabase owns homework status/dossier/publish state, Stripe owns payment events, Gmail owns correspondence. On any disagreement, the backend wins. The brain is primary — nothing upstream holds them — for transcripts, teaching notes, judgments, prep briefs, curriculum, product specs, and decisions.

**Do not read the wiki unless the task needs it.** Retrieval costs context every turn. Skip it for: code edits in `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/`; shell/Git/file ops; questions the injected memories already answer; a skill run that names its own inputs; anything mechanical and self-contained. Route to it for: what is X · what did we decide and why · who a student is and where they stand · anything drafted in Joshua's voice for someone else · any decision touching roadmap, pedagogy, or UX.

**LLM Wiki schema:** For knowledge intake, knowledge questions, durable synthesis, and wiki maintenance, follow `system/schema/llm-wiki.md`. Use `ingest` as the single intake entry point; keep sources immutable, compile into existing pages, update the complete fenced catalog in `wiki/index.md`, and append changes to `wiki/log.md`. Preserve domain homes and live-system authority. Mechanical tasks retain the retrieval exceptions above.

**Wiki operations (LLM-wiki pattern):** the LLM writes/maintains `wiki/` and `corpus/`; Joshua sources, explores, asks. Raw sources are immutable — read, never modify. *Ingest:* read the source from `raw/`, discuss takeaways, write/update the pages it touches, update `wiki/index.md`, append to `wiki/log.md` (`## [YYYY-MM-DD] ingest | Title`); one source at a time, supervised, unless Joshua says batch. *Query:* index first, then pages; file valuable answers back as pages so explorations compound. *Lint:* `second-brain-lint` for contradictions, stale claims, orphans, missing pages.

## Voice

- **No em-dashes, ever.** Joshua never types them; a stray em-dash reads as AI and costs trust.
- **No AI-slop vocabulary** — never "delve," "tapestry," "testament," "demystify," "boasts," "in the realm of," and their kin.
- **Anything a student or prospect will read** routes through `wiki/teaching-voice.md` and is drafted, never sent, unless it is a pre-approved template.
- **LSAT teaching:** use project-owned `lsat-u-voice` and `references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md`; this Joshua-selected standard takes precedence over older vendor voice blends.
- **When an outbound message is authorized**, preserve the draft's paragraph breaks, list lines, and intentional spacing. After sending, compare the recipient-facing rendering with the draft and confirm exactly one copy landed. Report any mismatch; never resend automatically.
- **Lead with your recommendation, not a menu.** Be decisive; don't over-hedge.
- **Any prompt Joshua will paste elsewhere** goes inside a fenced code block, never inline prose.

## Tools, keys, and secrets

- **The entire secret model is `.env` + `.gitignore`.** The root `.env` (git-ignored — verify with `git check-ignore .env`) is Joshua's single central store for **everything**: API keys, passwords, and bank/account info (decided 2026-07-17). OAuth client secrets live in `~/.config/gws/`, outside the repo. There is no vault, no Keychain, no encrypted store (`records.sparsebundle` is dead) and none is wanted. Never paste a credential into chat — add it to `.env` directly in the editor.
- **Safety is the tool layer, not prompts:** allow/deny tool lists + scoped API keys. Jay 2 acts under its own credentials and never impersonates Joshua.
- **Connected live systems:** Cal.com, Google Calendar, LSAT U Supabase, Stripe, Gmail. Full registry in `connections.md`.
- **Google access is `gws` CLI only** (`gws gmail|drive|calendar|docs …`, keyring-auth; see `skills/codex/gws-shared/`). Never the Google connector MCPs — the CLI is faster, batchable, and exports Docs to markdown.
- **Government IDs stay outside this OS.** Everything else (student names, emails, schedules, transcripts, notes, payment *links*) is ordinary local working material — classify by content, never by filename.
- **Git remote is enabled.** `origin` is GitHub (`vnjkoxpl-42905/ai-os`, decided 2026-09-29). The outer repo never tracks `.env`, `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` (own repo), runtime logs, or locks. Do not create gitlinks.
- **New laptop restore:** GitHub contains tracked ai-os files and skills. It deliberately excludes `.env`, `raw/`, app repos, credentials, and machine sessions. Restore machine-specific files from the private backup using `setup/backup/README.md` and its `README - START HERE.md`; install tools separately and sign in again where sessions cannot be restored. Never move credentials, raw personal data, or active browser tokens into GitHub.

## Skills

Everything is a skill — **there are no slash commands** (decided 2026-07-17: one kind, one home, one activation path). The catalog at `skills/` and `agents/` is inert storage; nothing auto-loads. An item runs only once **activated by copy** (not symlink) into `.agents/skills/` (Claude: `.claude/skills/`). Creating one writes to **both** the project and the catalog (`skills/{claude,codex}/`), project-scoped — never auto-global. `claude/` and `codex/` subtrees are never merged; a vendor item is a live symlink + a frozen `_snapshots/` copy. `system/scripts/check-skills.sh` validates the model; `skills/INDEX.md` indexes it.

Active in this root. Run `pickup` only when Joshua explicitly asks for it. Do not run it automatically at session start. Use `wrapup` at the end of substantive sessions:

| Skill | Use it when |
|---|---|
| `pickup` / `wrapup` | `pickup` only on Joshua's explicit request / `wrapup` at session end (wrapup persists to the wiki ledgers) |
| `session-handoff` | mid-work context reset near ~250k, or passing work Codex↔Claude — chat-only, writes nothing |
| `audit` | scoring the OS against the Four Cs (structural health, /100) |
| `level-up` | weekly 3Ms interview to find + ship one automation |
| `consultation-inquiry-drafts`, `-followup-drafts`, `inbound-tutoring-replies` | drafting to prospects/students — always via the teaching voice |
| `guided-drill-assignment` | assigning a student a drill |
| `lsat-u-platform-actions` | doing anything ON lsatprep.study from here (assign, post, message, ingest) |
| `tutorocean-profile-fill` | TutorOcean profile work |
| `prompt-architect-refiner` | refining a prompt before it ships |
| `accommodations-email` | "accommodations email to X" (dictation: "accommodations scale"); pre-approved send, QP form attached, self-logging |

When a skill run exposes a gap, improve the skill before moving on. Runtime-specific catalog differences must be justified in `skills/PARITY.md`; undeclared differences are defects.

## Guardrails

- **95% rule.** Make no changes until you are 95% confident what Joshua wants. Ask until you are.
- **Lovable credits require confirmation.** Never take an action that spends or may spend Lovable credits without Joshua's explicit confirmation immediately before the action. Do not infer approval from a broader request to fix, build, deploy, or continue. Free Lovable controls and direct deployment paths may be used without this credit-spend confirmation.
- **Lovable Cloud deploys through Lovable.** LSAT U currently uses a Lovable Cloud-managed backend. Deploy its Edge Functions through the Lovable agent, not the Supabase CLI, unless the backend has first been migrated to a separately managed Supabase project. Confirm this route with Joshua before starting a Lovable deployment.
- **Root stays thin.** Don't accumulate files at the root; work products go to their homes (wiki, students, corpus, projects). Anything left at root gets filed before the session ends.
- **Delegate bulk work.** Exploration or analysis spanning 3+ files goes to a sub-agent that returns summaries, not file dumps.
- **Shell ergonomics.** Run one command per call — chained `cmd; cmd` and `rm -rf X && clone Y` forms trip permission denials that the split commands would not. Never poll with `sleep`. Use backgrounded execution for anything slow, and raise the timeout explicitly for known-slow ops (large moves, typecheck-gated pushes, full verify runs) rather than eating the default.
- **Search before creating.** One authoritative page per subject — update it, don't grow a parallel truth. `wiki/index.md` catalogs every page and updates on every ingest.
- **Preserve provenance and source hashes.** Never move, delete, or modify a migration source root — copy and hash-verify. Never follow a source symlink outside its registered root.
- **Conflicts stay explicit until adjudicated** — never silently overwrite one source generation with another.
- **Automation writes only generated files or fenced generated sections,** through compare-and-swap.
- **Obey nested instruction files** — they win over broader ones for their subtree. LSATU's recovered `CLAUDE.md`/`AGENTS.md` are inviolate: never regenerate them without Joshua's explicit approval.
- **Memory = native automemory.** "Remember that…" writes one fact per file to the native store; `MEMORY.md` indexes it. This file is the rules; memory is learned facts — never mix them. Transcripts and raw captures never auto-promote.
- **Append meaningful system changes** to `wiki/log.md`; log locked decisions to `decisions/log.md`.

## Dual-runtime (Codex + Claude)

- Begin at the Git-root `AGENTS.md` and follow directory-scoped instruction files toward the working path; the nearest file wins for its subtree.
- Keep root instructions inside the runtime byte budget. Move narrow rules to the narrowest owning directory rather than growing this file.
- Activated skills are **copies** in `.agents/skills/` (from `skills/codex/`); agent definitions convert to `.codex/agents/*.toml` with the instructions in `developer_instructions`.
- Claude reads `CLAUDE.md` and never this file. Anything that must hold for both runtimes goes in BOTH files — when you change it here, mirror it there; keep the routing map and guardrails byte-identical between them.
- Activation is unequal by design: Codex reads copies in `.agents/skills/`, Claude reads copies in `.claude/skills/`. Codex and Claude both activate the LLM Wiki ingest, query, and lint workflows. Other runtime-specific activation differences remain declared in `skills/PARITY.md`. Never have separate runtimes writing the same page concurrently.
- Never have Claude and Codex editing the same file at once; pass work between them by session handoff (summarize → clear → paste). At ~250k tokens, hand off rather than riding autocompact.

This is the canonical voice doc: route all student-facing and brand copy through it. Built from a grill with Joshua (2026-06-13), then tested and enriched against the full corpus of his real correspondence (944 messages he authored, mid-2025 to mid-2026, from the Gmail takeout; 2026-07-18). Claims below marked with a quote are evidenced by that corpus.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt replaces the old two-register / 15-named-tools \\"preserve verbatim\\" profile (deleted 2026-06-10) and folds in the brand/marketing register the earlier draft was missing.\\r\\n\\r\\n**LSAT teaching standard, selected 2026-10-02:** Joshua chose the supplied Conditional Reasoning transcription as the reference for how LSAT material should sound and unfold. Read [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) before drafting lessons, notes, explanations, drills, flashcards, quizzes, or instructional feedback. It contains the detailed source analysis, original calibration examples, progression, and review rubric. [Source identity and limits](sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) are preserved separately. This choice develops the teaching-content register below; correspondence and marketing retain their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Core voice (every register)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe constant under all three registers below. This is Joshua on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Quiet authority.** State the value and let it land. Never oversell, never beg, never perform enthusiasm.\\r\\n- **Specific, never generic.** Real scores, the actual skill, the student\'s own history. Praise is a specific observation, not \\"great job.\\"\\r\\n- **Honest about the work, gentle about the person.** Be blunt that the LSAT is hard (\\"expect this to be exhausting and very challenging\\"). Never be blunt about a student\'s shortcomings. Honesty points at the material; warmth points at the person. When someone slips, nudge gently, never accuse.\\r\\n- **Light, grounded metaphor, understated in email.** In his real correspondence the recurring image is quiet: \\"partner with you on your LSAT journey,\\" \\"getting set up,\\" \\"foundational.\\" The vivid images (runway, climb, march toward November, an easy door to walk through) belong to authored teaching and brand copy, not to email. Keep metaphor light and grounded; never float off into abstraction.\\r\\n- **Balanced rhythm.** Air between ideas. Real correspondence is short and single-purpose (median ~280 characters). In teaching, vary sentence and paragraph length with the reasoning: give an explanation room, then let a short sentence settle the consequence. Do not impose email brevity on a lesson or cut its intermediate reasoning.\\r\\n- **Plain professional diction.** No corporate jargon, no startup hype (\\"unlock,\\" \\"crush,\\" \\"level up,\\" \\"game-changer\\"; \\"crush\\" appears once in 944 messages). Contractions are the norm, not the exception. Exclamation points are genuine, not performed: a warm message often carries one, occasionally more, especially in openers (\\"Sorry for such a long delay!\\"). He is not exclamation-averse.\\r\\n- **Forward-looking.** End on the next step or what\'s ahead.\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** Use periods, colons before lists, the middot (·) as a separator, and numbered steps for procedures.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Openings, closings, and signature phrases\\r\\n\\r\\nThe mechanical fingerprint of his email, from the real corpus. Match these when drafting as Joshua.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Greeting.** \\"Hi [Name],\\" is the default (used ~519x). \\"Hey [Name],\\" for established/returning students (~229x, warmer). \\"Hello [Name],\\" (~105x) and \\"Dear [Name],\\" (~18x, formal) for first contact or a more formal note.\\r\\n- **Sign-off.** \\"Best,\\" and \\"Best regards,\\" are the workhorses (~360x / ~237x). \\"Thanks,\\" when it\'s a favor or logistics (~146x). \\"Warmly,\\" / \\"Warm regards,\\" (~54x) for a genuinely warm note. \\"Talk soon,\\" (~9x) for someone he\'ll see again soon.\\r\\n- **Signature.** Almost always signed **\\"Joshua\\"** (844 of 944 messages). The name is near-universal; include it.\\r\\n- **Signature phrases (his verbal fingerprint, use naturally, don\'t overuse):** \\"let me know\\" (by far the most common, ~390x), \\"looking forward,\\" \\"I hope\\" / \\"hope all is well\\" / \\"hope you,\\" \\"no worries at all,\\" \\"happy to\\" / \\"more than happy to,\\" \\"just wanted to make sure,\\" \\"make sure,\\" \\"no rush,\\" \\"of course.\\"\\r\\n- **Punctuation he actually uses:** semicolons (correctly, ~41x); ellipses essentially never. (For em-dashes, the hard rule in Core voice governs: never.)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The apology / accountability move\\r\\n\\r\\nOne of his most frequent and characteristic behaviors, and worth naming on its own. When he is late to reply, or missed or mishandled something, he opens by owning it plainly, often with a brief honest reason, then pivots straight to the fix. No over-apologizing, no excuses dressed up.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'m so sorry. I thought I had let you know ... I clearly didn\'t communicate that as well as I should have.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'ve been away from work for personal reasons, but I\'m back now and getting caught up.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe pattern: own it in the first line, give the real reason in one clause, move to what happens next. Warm, candid, brief.\\r\\n\\r\\n## When something on his side broke (approved shape, 2026-09-11)\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom the Sandra booking-error reply, edited by Joshua line by line over three drafts and then sent as-is. Use this shape whenever a student hit a bug or a mistake that was his. The sent text is the reference:\\r\\n\\r\\n> Hi Sandra,\\r\\n>\\r\\n> I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet. The page was showing a few times that the system then refused, and it looks like you landed on those. That is fixed now.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Your link is the same one as before:\\r\\n> [link]\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Pick any time it shows and it will book. If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Once again, my apologies.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Best,\\r\\n> Joshua\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Apology first, as the opening line.** \\"I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\" The ownership sits in the first two sentences, before any explanation.\\r\\n2. **State their position plainly, as a confirmation.** \\"To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet.\\" Answer the question they actually asked. Don\'t mention the package or credits unless the situation calls for it.\\r\\n3. **One-sentence explanation, then \\"That is fixed now.\\"** No mechanics, no system names.\\r\\n4. **Hand them the next step, plus the manual fallback.** The link, \\"pick any time it shows and it will book,\\" and the offer to do it by hand: \\"If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\"\\r\\n5. **Invite the next report:** \\"If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\"\\r\\n6. **Close with \\"Once again, my apologies.\\"** then \\"Best,\\" and \\"Joshua\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nCut from the draft, do not reuse:\\r\\n- \\"Sorry for the runaround\\" as a sign-off. The apology opens the email; it is not a closing quip.\\r\\n- \\"You will get a calendar invite with the Google Meet link right after.\\" Do not narrate what the system will send; it reads as a promise about machinery.\\r\\n- \\"You have all 10 sessions\\" / \\"nothing was used from your package.\\" Leave the package out unless it is needed; the reassurance they want is that nothing is booked, not an accounting.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Situational micro-templates\\r\\n\\r\\nVerbatim-grounded reference lines for the situations that recur most. Adapt, never paste blindly.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Student reschedules / cancels:** low-pressure, no guilt.\\r\\n  > No worries at all. It happens, so please don\'t worry about it. If you would like to reschedule, just let me know.\\r\\n- **He missed / no-showed (approved make-good template):** full ownership, real reason, offer to make it right (see the apology move above). When the cause was an unexpected health issue, the approved shape is: sincerely apologize, state that a serious health scare unexpectedly took him out of commission, offer a **completely free, full LSAT session**, and hand them the complimentary booking link `https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/complimentary-session`. Emphasis stays limited: bold the complimentary offer, italicize the line that one focused session can put points on the board. Draft in Gmail; never send without Joshua\'s explicit go-ahead.\\r\\n- **Consultation reminder (day-of):**\\r\\n  > Just checking in before our consultation today. Looking forward to speaking with you. I just wanted to make sure everything is still good.\\r\\n- **Consultation follow-up:** warm, specific to their situation.\\r\\n  > Really nice meeting you. I enjoyed our conversation ... I\'m glad we got to talk through where you\'re at and what you\'re aiming for on test day.\\r\\n- **Inbound prospect (hasn\'t booked):** state availability plainly, no overselling, hand them the next step.\\r\\n  > Thanks for reaching out ... I\'d be happy to talk more about what you\'re working on and see if we\'d be a good fit. You can schedule a consultation here.\\r\\n- **Student under real-life stress:** meet the person first.\\r\\n  > Please don\'t apologize ... it sounds like you are dealing with an incredibly stressful and demanding situation.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The three registers\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich voice governs which surface.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. Coaching (earnest) -- inbox, personal encouragement, email\\r\\n\\r\\nFully earnest. Warm, sincere, calm, patient. No jokes. The closest thing to being in the room with Joshua. Normalizes struggle, then moves to the work.\\r\\n\\r\\nReference line (target tone):\\r\\n\\r\\n> Totally normal to trip on this one. Let\'s slow down: what is the author actually concluding? Once you see it, the trap answers lose their grip. You\'ve got this.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Encouragement (student missed a hard one):** honest first, then forward. Name that it\'s genuinely hard, stay calm, move to t…12546 tokens truncated…l the blue sticker the **sufficient condition**. Sufficient means enough. Here, enough to establish inspection under this rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Inspection is the **necessary condition**. A blue-sticker parcel has to meet that requirement. Otherwise it would be an exception to a rule that allows no exceptions.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now suppose a parcel has been inspected. Does it have to have a blue sticker?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We still don\'t know. Inspection was required for the blue-sticker parcels. The shop never said those were the only parcels it inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis returns to meaning at every term. The example does not assert why the shop uses stickers or when they are applied. Those extra facts are unnecessary.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Teach a necessary expression through its whole meaning\\r\\n\\r\\n> Editing this newsletter requires an account.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> What does the sentence say the editor needs? An account. Keep that complete idea on the required side of the relationship:\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **Edits this newsletter → Has an account**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Read it back: if someone edits this newsletter, they have an account. That matches the sentence.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Try the reverse. If someone has an account, do they necessarily edit the newsletter? The sentence hasn\'t told us that. An account could belong to someone who only reads it.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe final possible counterexample is introduced as something consistent with the sentence, not an asserted fact about this system. Technical placement has a semantic reason.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Give a flashcard a reasoning task\\r\\n\\r\\n**Front**\\r\\n\\r\\n> Every package in Locker 4 has been scanned. A package has been scanned. Must it be in Locker 4? Why?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Back**\\r\\n\\r\\n> No. The rule tells us about packages already in Locker 4. It doesn\'t say every scanned package goes there.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is more useful than a card that only asks “Necessary condition = which side?” Keep simple recall cards when recall is the actual skill; do not mistake that for complete reasoning practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Make feedback identify the missed connection\\r\\n\\r\\n> You used the sentence in the reverse direction. “Only registered visitors enter the archive” requires registration for entry. It hasn\'t said every registered visitor enters. Read the diagram as **Enters → Registered** and compare it with the sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback names the mistake, shows the relevant meaning, and gives a repair. It avoids generic praise, mockery, and labels such as “weak reasoning.”\\r\\n\\r\\n### Explain an ordinary learning objection\\r\\n\\r\\n> “I understood the sentence. Why write it down?”\\r\\n>\\r\\n> With one short rule, you may not need to. When a passage gives you several relationships, a diagram lets you compare them without rereading the wording each time. In this exercise, write the relationship so we can check that you kept its direction and complete ideas.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe answer gives a decision criterion and distinguishes the purpose of this exercise from a blanket rule about every test question.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Transfer the voice to another LSAT topic\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conclusion is the claim the author is asking you to accept. The other statements are supposed to support it.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Nora says, “The library should stay open later because every evening seat was taken this week.” What is she trying to persuade us of?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That the library should stay open later. The full seats are her reason. She expects that reason to support the proposed change.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You don\'t have to agree with her. First, separate what she wants you to accept from what she gives you in support.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe same mechanism works outside conditionals: definition, small example, question, explanation, and a useful boundary. No conditional topic has been imported into the conclusion lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Revise the draft in a useful order\\r\\n\\r\\n### First, check the reasoning and scope\\r\\n\\r\\nList what is given, what follows, and why. Try to construct a case that satisfies the given information but makes the proposed conclusion false. If that case is possible, revise the logic before polishing the voice. Check modality, negation, quantifier scope, and whether the learner has actually been taught every required operation.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor a rewrite, preserve Joshua\'s established method and explicit lesson scope. The new reference governs delivery. It does not authorize removing content that students need or adding source topics the current assignment does not cover.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Then, check the learner\'s experience\\r\\n\\r\\nRead as someone who has never seen the notation. Can they tell what the concept is? Can they answer the first question with what the page has taught? Does each new term refer to an idea they have seen? Does the page tell them why the correct answer follows?\\r\\n\\r\\nPut the explanation beside the point where it is needed. Do not rely on an interaction to imply a distinction the prose never explains. A student who cannot use the animation must still be able to learn from the text.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Then, listen to the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nRead aloud at an ordinary teaching pace. Replace stiff phrasing with the words the teacher would naturally use. Keep the intermediate thoughts. Vary sentence length where the task changes. Remove repeated catchphrases, inflated enthusiasm, branded metaphors, and irrelevant asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not repair generic prose by scattering “Cool,” “Let\'s,” or “That\'s it” through it. Repair the missing reasoning, the abrupt transition, or the abstract example.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Finally, verify the student surface\\r\\n\\r\\nCheck the actual prompt, answer state, labels, buttons, diagrams, and explanations. A good note can be undermined by a shell that exposes internal planning or reveals the answer too soon. Preview desktop and mobile when the interface changes. Keep the instructor\'s planning map separate from what the learner sees.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Use this rubric before handing over LSAT writing\\r\\n\\r\\nScore each dimension from 0 to 2: **0** absent or misleading; **1** partly present but needing revision; **2** clear and usable. This is an editorial tool, not a validated learning measure. A logical error or scope violation requires revision regardless of the total.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Dimension | What a strong draft does |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| **Ordinary meaning** | Defines the idea in language the reader can understand before notation. |\\r\\n| **Concrete anchor** | Uses an original, small, unambiguous situation and states needed assumptions. |\\r\\n| **Visible reasoning** | Shows the intermediate connection instead of asserting a result. |\\r\\n| **Point of view** | Keeps the reader involved; uses teacher narration purposefully. |\\r\\n| **Cadence** | Gives complex thoughts room and short conclusions a clear place to land. |\\r\\n| **Vocabulary** | Keeps useful technical terms, explains them, and avoids internal jargon. |\\r\\n| **Questions and pauses** | Asks a genuine decision and gives an appropriate chance to answer. |\\r\\n| **Boundaries** | Shows what the information leaves open and why a tempting move fails. |\\r\\n| **Progression** | Each operation has a prerequisite, a reason to appear, and adequate practice. |\\r\\n| **Transfer** | Fresh questions require the taught skill, with reasoned feedback. |\\r\\n| **Tone and warmth** | Expects effort, supports the next action, and never mocks uncertainty. |\\r\\n| **Presentation** | Examples, comparisons, notation, and interactions clarify the reasoning. |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not treat a high total as proof of an identical voice. Use the original source and Joshua\'s response to calibrate the subjective feel. His concrete corrections override a score.\\r\\n\\r\\n## A reusable writing brief\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this when giving another writer or agent the task. Supply the actual source, lesson scope, prerequisite knowledge, and approved method along with it.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nWrite this LSAT material in Joshua\'s selected teaching voice.\\r\\nRead wiki/teaching-voice.md, references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md,\\r\\nand wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md before drafting.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the reader beside the teacher. Start with the ordinary meaning of\\r\\nthe idea, then make it concrete with an original, intuitive example.\\r\\nShow each small inference in natural language. Ask the question a learner\\r\\nwould actually need to answer here. When the question is practice, leave\\r\\nroom for an attempt before revealing the reasoning.\\r\\n\\r\\nVary sentence length by purpose. Let explanations breathe and use short\\r\\nsentences to settle a consequence or a limit. Use contractions naturally.\\r\\nTeach the technical term when there is a clear idea to attach it to.\\r\\nRepeat an anchor only when the repetition changes the learner\'s view.\\r\\nShow a tempting mistake and how to detect it when that distinction matters.\\r\\n\\r\\nPreserve Joshua\'s method, the exact logical strength, complete conditions,\\r\\nand the selected topic scope. Give fresh practice with explained answers.\\r\\nDo not copy the reference\'s examples, jokes, personal claims, or procedural\\r\\ncounts. No em dashes, hype, invented evidence, internal stage language,\\r\\nor decorative catchphrases. Light humor is optional and must help the lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the result aloud, check every inference, and review the student-facing\\r\\nprompt and answer states. If the reader cannot hear why the next thought\\r\\nfollows, revise the explanation before polishing the surface.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Keep this reference current\\r\\n\\r\\nThis file is the detailed method reference, not a second canonical brand voice. [Teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md) remains the router. [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md) remains the record of demonstrated wording failures. Add Joshua\'s later calibration to the relevant section here rather than creating competing LSAT voice documents.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe project-owned `lsat-u-voice` skill reads this standard. Recap and bootcamp workflows also read it explicitly. An older vendor voice skill describes a different writer blend; it must not override this Joshua-selected standard in this project. Managed plugin caches are preserved rather than silently rewritten.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source archive is immutable. Future evidence or revised attachments should be recorded as a separate generation with provenance, not written over the original.\\r\\n"}'}]

A booking alone doesn\'t put you there.\\",\\r\\n    readback: \'If someone eats on the terrace, they have a booked table.\',\\r\\n  },\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    sentence: \'The only phones placed in the same-day repair queue are phones that arrived with a cracked screen.\', word: \'the only\', family: \'sufficient\',\\r\\n    target: \'phones placed in the same-day repair queue\', partial: \'phones\', other: \'phones that arrived with a cracked screen\',\\r\\n    left: \'Phone in same-day repair queue\', right: \'Arrived with cracked screen\',\\r\\n    partialFeedback: \'Phones alone would make a claim about every phone in the shop. Phones placed in the same-day repair queue.\',\\r\\n    reversed: \'Your arrow puts every phone that arrived with a cracked screen in the same-day repair queue. Any phone placed in the same-day repair queue arrived with a cracked screen. Arriving with a cracked screen doesn\'t put a phone in that queue.\\",\\r\\n    readback: \'If a phone is placed in the same-day repair queue, it arrived with a cracked screen.\',\\r\\n  },\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    sentence: \'Only stalls with a permit sell food at the festival.\', word: \'only\', family: \'necessary\',\\r\\n    target: \'stalls with a permit\', partial: \'stalls\', other: \'sell food at the festival\',\\r\\n    left: \'Stall sells food at festival\', right: \'Stall has permit\',\\r\\n    partialFeedback: \'Stalls alone loses the restriction. [item.right, item.left] : [item.left, item.right];\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction MixedItemCheck({ item, index, onChecked }: { item: MixedItem; index: number; onChecked: (checked: boolean) => void }) {\\r\\n  const [draftDiagram, setDraftDiagram] = useState(\'\');\\r\\n  const [draftReadback, setDraftReadback] = useState(\'\');\\r\\n  const [comparing, setComparing] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const draftQuestion = useId();\\r\\n  const readbackQuestion = useId();\\r\\n  const draftInput = useRef<HTMLTextAreaElement>(null);\\r\\n  const [target, setTarget] = useState<string | null>(null);\\r\\n  const [left, setLeft] = useState<string | null>(null);\\r\\n  const [checked, setChecked] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const targetQuestion = useId();\\r\\n  const sideQuestion = useId();\\r\\n  const change = (update: () => void) => { update(); setChecked(false); onChecked(false); };\\r\\n  const reset = () => { draftInput.current?.focus(); setComparing(false); setTarget(null); setLeft(null); setChecked(false); onChecked(false); };\\r\\n  const targetCorrect = target === item.target;\\r\\n  const sideCorrect = left === item.left;\\r\\n  const number = index + 1;\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-lab co-check\\" data-mixed-item={number}>\\r\\n    <blockquote>{item.sentence}</blockquote>\\r\\n    <p>Write the complete conditions and the arrow yourself. Then read your diagram back as an if/then sentence. You can use words or short labels you define.</p>\\r\\n    <label htmlFor={draftQuestion}>Your diagram for sentence {number}</label>\\r\\n    <textarea ref={draftInput} id={draftQuestion} className=\\"co-attempt-input\\" rows={3} value={draftDiagram} readOnly={comparing} onChange={event => setDraftDiagram(event.target.value)}/>\\r\\n    <label htmlFor={readbackQuestion}>Your English readback for sentence {number}</label>\\r\\n    <textarea id={readbackQuestion} className=\\"co-attempt-input\\" rows={3} value={draftReadback} readOnly={comparing} onChange={event => setDraftReadback(event.target.value)}/>\\r\\n    {!comparing && <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"co-primary\\" disabled={!draftDiagram.trim() || !draftReadback.trim()} onClick={() => setComparing(true)}>Compare my translation for sentence {number}</button>}\\r\\n    {comparing && <>\\r\\n    <p>Your written answer is saved for you to compare. The choices below check the target and direction; your written answer is not graded automatically.</p>\\r\\n    <h3 id={targetQuestion}>What does <strong>{item.word}</strong> refer to? Choose the complete idea.</h3>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-choices\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-labelledby={targetQuestion}>{targetOptions(item, index).map(option => <button type=\\"button\\" key={option} aria-pressed={target === option} onClick={() => change(() => setTarget(option))}>{option}</button>)}</div>\\r\\n    <h3 id={sideQuestion}>Which complete idea goes on the left of the must arrow?</h3>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-choices\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-labelledby={sideQuestion}>{sideOptions(item, index).map(option => <button type=\\"button\\" key={option} aria-pressed={left === option} onClick={() => change(() => setLeft(option))}>{option}</button>)}</div>\\r\\n    {left && <RuleDiagram left={left} right={left === item.left ? item.right : item.left} label={`Your diagram: ${left} implies ${left === item.left ? item.right : item.left}`}/>}\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-control-row\\"><button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"co-primary\\" disabled={target === null || left === null} onClick={() => { setChecked(true); onChecked(true); }}>Check sentence {number}</button><button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"cr-reset\\" onClick={reset}>Try sentence {number} again</button></div>\\r\\n    <div role=\\"status\\">{checked && <div className={`co-feedback ${targetCorrect && sideCorrect ? \'co-correct\' : \'\'}`}>\\r\\n      <strong>{targetCorrect && sideCorrect ? \'Your target and arrow choices preserve the rule.\' : \'Revise the part described below.\'}</strong>\\r\\n      <p>{targetCorrect ? `You found the complete target: ${item.target}.` : target === item.partial ? item.partialFeedback : item.otherFeedback}</p>\\r\\n      <p>{sideCorrect ? \'Your arrow keeps the direction the sentence gives.\' : item.reversed}</p>\\r\\n      <p>Here, <strong>{item.word}</strong> points to what is {item.family === \'sufficient\' ? \'enough, so its target goes on the left\' : \'required, so its target goes on the right\'}. {item.why}</p>\\r\\n      <p>Read it back: {item.readback}</p>\\r\\n    </div>}</div>\\r\\n    </>}\\r\\n    {!comparing && <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"cr-reset\\" onClick={reset}>Try sentence {number} again</button>}\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function MixedFamilyPractice() {\\r\\n  const [current, setCurrent] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [checked, setChecked] = useState<boolean[]>(() => mixedFamilyItems.map(() => false));\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    <p>These sentences use both kinds of expression. The family name stays hidden until you check your choices. First translate each sentence yourself, without supplied condition labels. Then compare your work and check the complete target and arrow direction before moving on.</p>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-kicker\\" role=\\"status\\">Sentence {current + 1} of {mixedFamilyItems.length}</p>\\r\\n    {mixedFamilyItems.map((item, index) => <div key={item.sentence} hidden={current !== index}><MixedItemCheck item={item} index={index} onChecked={value => setChecked(previous => previous.map((old, itemIndex) => itemIndex === index ? value : old))}/></div>)}\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-choices\\"><button type=\\"button\\" disabled={current === 0} onClick={() => setCurrent(current - 1)}>Previous mixed sentence</button><button type=\\"button\\" disabled={current === mixedFamilyItems.length - 1 || !checked[current]} onClick={() => setCurrent(current + 1)}>Next mixed sentence</button></div>\\r\\n    <p>Moving between sentences keeps your written attempt, choices, and feedback. Trying a sentence again keeps your writing so you can revise it, and hides the supplied labels until you compare again.</p>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n"}'}]

Having the required result does not establish the sufficient condition.\'] },\\r\\n];\\r\\nfunction FourCaseExplorer() {\\r\\n  const [selected, setSelected] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [answers, setAnswers] = useState<Record<number, number>>({});\\r\\n  const current = cases[selected];\\r\\n  const answer = answers[selected];\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"cr-cases co-lab\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">One rule. Four starting facts.</p><blockquote>{raffleRule}</blockquote>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-case-tabs\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-label=\\"Choose a starting fact\\">{cases.map((item, index) => <button type=\\"button\\" key={item.label} aria-pressed={selected === index} onClick={() => setSelected(index)}><span>{String(index + 1).padStart(2, \'0\')}</span>{item.label}</button>)}</div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-case-fact\\"><span>Given</span><strong>{current.fact}</strong></div><h3>{current.question}</h3>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-answer-options\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-label=\\"Choose what follows\\">{current.options.map((option, index) => <button type=\\"button\\" key={option} aria-pressed={answer === index} onClick={() => setAnswers({ ...answers, [selected]: index })}>{option}</button>)}</div>\\r\\n    <div role=\\"status\\">{answer !== undefined && <div className={`cr-feedback ${answer === current.correct ? \'cr-correct\' : \'cr-revise\'}`}><strong>{answer === current.correct ? \'Correct.\' : \'Not quite.\'}</strong><p>{current.reasoning[answer]}</p></div>}</div>\\r\\n    {Object.keys(answers).length === 4 && <div className=\\"cr-case-review\\"><h3>Keep these four distinctions</h3><dl>{cases.map(item => <div key={item.label}><dt>{item.label}</dt><dd>{item.options[item.correct]}</dd></div>)}</dl><p>The rule still holds in all four cases. Two facts give an inference; two leave the other condition open.</p></div>}\\r\\n    <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"cr-reset\\" onClick={() => { setAnswers({}); setSelected(0); }}>Reset the four cases</button>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction ContrapositiveDiscovery() {\\r\\n  const [attempted, setAttempted] = useState(false);\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"cr-discovery\\"><p>Use this stated rule for a different case.</p><blockquote>If a report is in the published archive, it has an approval stamp.</blockquote>\\r\\n    <p>A report in that archive has to carry the stamp. Now you inspect Report K. It has no approval stamp.</p>\\r\\n    <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Could Report K be in the published archive while this rule holds?\\" options={[\'Yes, its location is unrestricted.\', \'No, it cannot be in that archive.\', \'Yes, the missing stamp puts it in the archive.\']} correct={1} onAttempt={choice => setAttempted(choice !== null)} feedback={[\'Its location is restricted by the rule. If K were in the archive, it would have a stamp, which conflicts with the given fact.\', \'An archived K would have to have a stamp. K has no stamp, so it cannot be in the published archive under this rule.\', \'The missing stamp rules out archive membership. It does not establish it.\']}/>\\r\\n    {attempted && <div className=\\"cr-discovery-reasoning\\"><p>That way of using the rule is the <strong>contrapositive</strong>: when the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition cannot be present.</p>\\r\\n      <p>It is the same relationship. We didn\'t add a second rule; we worked out a consequence already contained in the first one.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>{raffleRule}</blockquote>\\r\\n      <p>Here, “you don\'t get a bicycle” means you never receive the promised outcome. It does not mean you\'re still waiting for a prize that will arrive later.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"You don\'t get a bicycle. What follows about winning the raffle?\\" options={[\\"You don\'t win the raffle.\\", \'You win the raffle.\', \'Nothing follows about winning.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Winning would require getting a bicycle. Since that outcome does not occur, you cannot have won under the rule.\', \'Winning without getting a bicycle would break the rule. The absent requirement excludes winning.\', \'The missing requirement does give an inference. If you won, you would get a bicycle.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Don\'t confuse that with starting from a requirement that is present. You could get a bicycle as a gift without winning. Let\'s compare all four starting facts.</p>\\r\\n    </div>}\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function RuleDiagram({ left, right, label }: { left: string; right: string; label?: string }) {\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"cr-diagram\\" role=\\"img\\" aria-label={label || `${left} implies ${right}`}><strong>{left}</strong><span aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</span><strong>{right}</strong></div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\nconst writingSteps = [\\r\\n  { button: \'Find the indicator\', explanation: \'I see if. It introduces the sufficient condition: you win the raffle.\' },\\r\\n  { button: \'Draw the must arrow\', explanation: \'The arrow connects the sufficient condition to the necessary condition. It means that when the left holds, the right has to hold.\' },\\r\\n  { button: \'Place the complete target\', explanation: \'Win the raffle is the complete sufficient condition. I shorten it to Win and put it on the left. Win means winning this raffle, not any other contest.\' },\\r\\n  { button: \'Fill the remaining condition\', explanation: \'The other condition is that you get a bicycle. I shorten it to Get bicycle and put it on the right. Keep getting a bicycle distinct from merely owning one already.\' },\\r\\n];\\r\\nexport function IncrementalWriter() {\\r\\n  const [step, setStep] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const resetControl = useRef<HTMLButtonElement>(null);\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"cr-writer co-lab\\"><blockquote>{step > 0 ? <><mark>If</mark> you win the raffle, you get a bicycle.</> : raffleRule}</blockquote>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-writing-sheet\\" aria-label=\\"Diagram construction\\"><span className=\\"cr-paper-label\\">Our diagram</span>{step < 2 ? <p>The sentence is our starting point.</p> : <RuleDiagram left={step >= 3 ? \'Win\' : \'Not placed yet\'} right={step >= 4 ? \'Get bicycle\' : \'Not placed yet\'} />}</div>\\r\\n    <div role=\\"status\\">{step > 0 && <div className=\\"cr-feedback\\"><strong>Step {step} of 4</strong><p>{writingSteps[step - 1].explanation}</p>{step === 4 && <p>Read it back: if you win the raffle, you get a bicycle. We shortened the words and kept the relationship.</p>}</div>}</div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-control-row\\">{step < 4 && <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"cr-primary\\" onClick={() => { if (step === 3) resetControl.current?.focus(); …4803 tokens truncated…ast\\"><div><h3>Left: sufficient</h3><p>This is the condition that\'s enough to establish the other one. Group 1 expressions such as <strong>if, when, all, every</strong> and <strong>any</strong> can identify this condition.</p><p>“All people who borrow a locker have a key.” All refers to <strong>people who borrow a locker</strong>. For someone in that group, having a key follows. Borrowing goes left.</p></div><div><h3>Right: necessary</h3><p>This is what the sufficient condition requires. Group 2 expressions such as <strong>only if, requires</strong> and <strong>must</strong> can identify the requirement.</p><p>“Borrowing a locker requires a key.” What is required? <strong>Having a key.</strong> That condition goes right.</p></div></div>\\r\\n      <RuleDiagram left=\\"Borrows a locker\\" right=\\"Has a key\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>Read it back: if someone borrows a locker, they have a key. The arrow records that connection. It hasn\'t told us that everyone with a key borrows a locker.</p>\\r\\n      <h3>The sentence can move. The roles stay the same.</h3>\\r\\n      <p>Compare these two versions of our raffle rule. The indicator is bold; the complete idea it refers to is in brackets.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p><strong>If</strong> [you win the raffle], you get a bicycle.</p><p>You get a bicycle <strong>if</strong> [you win the raffle].</p><span>If identifies the sufficient condition in both sentences.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>In the second sentence, if appears in the middle. Its condition comes last, but winning is still enough to establish getting a bicycle. That condition still goes left. Don\'t let the first phrase in English choose the first side of your diagram.</p>\\r\\n      <RuleDiagram left=\\"Win the raffle\\" right=\\"Get a bicycle\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>Now try the same move with a requirement.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p><strong>Only</strong> [people with a key] borrow a locker.</p><p>A person borrows a locker <strong>only if</strong> [they have a key].</p><span>Only and only if identify the necessary condition here.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>Both sentences say locker borrowers have a key. In the first, only is at the beginning. In the second, only if is in the middle. Having a key is required in both, so it goes right. <strong>If</strong> and <strong>only if</strong> have different jobs; read only if as the whole expression.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"A person borrows a locker only if they have a key. Which condition goes on the right?\\" options={[\'Borrows a locker\', \'Has a key\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Borrowing is what has the requirement. Ask what a locker borrower must have: a key. Borrowing goes left; having a key goes right.\', \'Having a key is required for borrowing. Only if appears in the middle, but it still identifies the necessary condition.\']}/>\\r\\n      <h3>The indicator can also follow its target</h3>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>[Having a key] is <strong>required</strong> for borrowing a locker.</p><p>Borrowing a locker <strong>requires</strong> [having a key].</p><span>Having a key is the requirement in both sentences.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>The target is the complete idea the indicator refers to. It comes before required in the first sentence and after requires in the second. Having a key still goes right. We find it by asking what is required, not by taking whichever words come next.</p>\\r\\n      <p>You\'ll work through the less familiar expressions in their Group 1 and Group 2 lessons. For each one, keep the whole sentence: what is enough, and what does it require?</p>\\r\\n      <p>Let\'s build an original diagram slowly. First find the indicator, then draw the arrow, place the complete idea it refers to, and fill the other side.</p>\\r\\n      <IncrementalWriter/>\\r\\n      <p>Now move the if-clause: “You get a bicycle if you win the raffle.” The winning condition comes last in English but still goes left in the diagram. Its job has not changed.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Which diagram preserves the moved if-clause?\\" options={[\'Get bicycle → Win\', \'Win → Get bicycle\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You let sentence order choose the arrow. The if-clause still makes winning enough for getting a bicycle.\', \'The sufficient condition is still winning. Where it appears in the sentence does not change what it establishes.\']}/>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={9} label=\\"Write and read the contrapositive\\" title=\\"Write the meaning you already understand\\">\\r\\n      <p>Win means you win the raffle. Get bicycle means you get a bicycle. Our original is Win → Get bicycle.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We already know that not getting a bicycle rules out winning. Write that as <strong>~Get bicycle → ~Win</strong>. The <strong>~</strong> sign means <strong>not</strong>. Not getting the outcome is different from not having received it yet.</p>\\r\\n      <p>To write a contrapositive from a diagram, reverse the two sides and negate <strong>both complete conditions</strong>. A condition already containing not loses that not when we negate it. Let\'s try all four patterns.</p>\\r\\n      <SignPatterns/>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-method\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">The complete five-step routine</p><ol><li><strong>Find the indicator.</strong> Read its job in the whole sentence.</li><li><strong>Identify both complete ideas.</strong> Keep restrictions such as current or submitted by Friday.</li><li><strong>Place the roles.</strong> Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. Choose labels you can read back.</li><li><strong>Write the contrapositive.</strong> Reverse the sides and negate both complete conditions.</li><li><strong>Read both in English.</strong> Compare them with what the original sentence actually requires.</li></ol></div>\\r\\n      <p>The original and its contrapositive describe the same relationship. If writing both helps you see that relationship, write both. If you can read the contrapositive from the original when it is relevant, you needn\'t copy it merely to have a second line.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Read an arrow forward to use its stated direction. To read its contrapositive, start from the right condition\'s absence and work back to the left condition\'s absence. That is a different move from reading the arrow backward unchanged.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={10} label=\\"Practical diagramming questions\\" title=\\"Use writing to make your reasoning easier to check\\">\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-faq\\">\\r\\n        <details><summary>When should I diagram?</summary><p>While learning, write enough to make your choices visible. A difficult sentence, several rules or a connection you keep rereading can be a reason to put the relationship on paper. With practice, you may hold a simple rule mentally. Use the writing to help the reasoning.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>How do I check whether my diagram is right?</summary><p>Read it back and compare the whole meaning. Did you keep each restriction? Does your arrow promise something the original leaves open? When a diagram is wrong, record the exact phrase and the mistake it produced. That gives you something specific to practice. Familiarity alone is not proof of correctness.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>What if writing takes time?</summary><p>Start by producing a correct, readable diagram. Speed is a later practice goal. Writing can reduce rereading, but it does not save time in every case. Compare how it helps on the sentences you are actually working through. First make the relationship accurate. Work on speed once you can explain why the arrow follows. </p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>Should I always write the contrapositive?</summary><p>You need to understand it every time. Write it when it helps you use the missing requirement, inspect your translation or connect rules. Otherwise, you may read that direction from the original. Extra notation should have a job.</p></details>\\r\\n        <aside className=\\"cr-training-note\\"><h3>Why this practice asks for both</h3><p>In the integrated drill, write or choose the original and the contrapositive. We\'re checking that you can produce both. Once that skill is reliable, a performance task need not require both lines for every rule.</p></aside>\\r\\n        <details><summary>How should I abbreviate the conditions?</summary><p>Use short labels that bring back the complete idea. W might mean win this raffle. Current pass may be clearer than a single letter if you\'re tracking several kinds of pass. Shortening should save writing without losing meaning. There is no fixed letter limit.</p></details>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Only envelopes with a paid stamp go into this tray. Someone wrote Paid stamp → In tray. What needs repairing?\\" options={[\'The labels need to be longer.\', \'The arrow makes the requirement enough.\', \'Nothing; the first phrase always goes left.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Longer labels would not repair the direction. The tray requires a paid stamp, but a stamped envelope can remain outside it.\', \'Only restricts tray envelopes to ones with paid stamps. In tray → Paid stamp preserves that requirement; the proposed reverse promises tray placement to every stamped envelope.\', \'Sentence order does not determine diagram order. Only identifies the paid stamp as required for tray placement.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>That error came from the language. Let\'s inspect precisely which idea an indicator refers to.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={11} label=\\"Find the target\\" title=\\"Read the whole idea the indicator refers to\\">\\r\\n      <p>An indicator\'s <strong>target</strong> is the complete word or phrase it refers to. In “All guests wearing blue badges receive vouchers,” all refers to guests wearing blue badges, not guests alone.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The target often comes after its indicator. But often isn\'t always. Try these sentences in order.</p>\\r\\n      {targetExamples.map(item => <TargetReader item={item} key={item.label}/>)}\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>“Editor approval is required for publication.”</p><p>“Publication requires editor approval.”</p><span>Same requirement, different placement</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>Neither the closest noun nor the next word is a reliable target rule. Read which complete idea the sentence calls required. Then give that idea its role in the diagram.</p>\\r\\n      <p>When the wording still feels uncertain, we can test a proposed translation against an imagined case.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={12} label=\\"Check an interpretation\\" title=\\"Try the sentence from the inside\\">\\r\\n      <p>A <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong> lets you test what a proposed sufficient condition would establish. First read the ordinary meaning. Propose the sufficient idea. Imagine a case meeting it. Ask what the sentence now requires.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>Only people wearing helmets ride the studio bikes.</blockquote><p>The sentence restricts riders to people wearing helmets. Keep that meaning while trying each proposed starting point.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-personalized\\"><div><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">First proposed starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"If I\'m riding a studio bike, what does this sentence tell me?\\" options={[\'I am wearing a helmet.\', \'The helmet question is open.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'The sentence puts every rider in the helmet-wearing group. Riding → Wearing helmet preserves that meaning.\', \'Only restricts who rides. If you meet the riding condition, the sentence does settle helmet wearing.\']}/></div><div><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Try the other starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"If I\'m wearing a helmet, must I be riding a studio bike?\\" options={[\'Yes, the helmet proves riding.\', \'No, I could wear one without riding.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You gave every helmet wearer a ride. The sentence restricts riders; it does not make all helmet wearers riders.\', \'A helmet wearer waiting beside a bike fits the sentence. Wearing helmet → Riding would add a promise the original did not make.\']}/></div></div>\\r\\n      <p>The first reading matches the sentence. The second proposed arrow fails because it rules out a case the original permits. Use the imagined situation to inspect meaning, not to supply extra facts from what usually happens.</p>\\r\\n      <h3>Use the What Test beside it</h3><p>Ask a sensible question about the expression: only what? People wearing helmets. What is required? Wearing a helmet. What is enough? Riding a studio bike, under this rule, establishes that requirement.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The What Test finds the complete target. The Personalized If/Then checks what your proposed direction says about a case. Use them together when one part of the translation is unclear.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We\'ve now read the relationship, understood its limits, written both directions and checked the language. Put those pieces together with fresh sentences.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={13} label=\\"Use the whole process\\" title=\\"Build an original and its contrapositive\\">\\r\\n      <p>For each sentence, identify the indicator and both complete conditions. Choose the original diagram, then its contrapositive. Read both back before checking.</p>\\r\\n      <p>You can look back. The job here is to produce and inspect the reasoning, not to prove you memorized every expression on the first reading. The labels below keep the complete ideas visible while you choose the directions.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-integrated-grid\\">{referencePracticeItems.map((item, index) => <IntegratedItem item={item} number={index + 1} key={item.sentence}/>)}</div>\\r\\n      <p>If an answer needs revision, name the exact issue: lost qualification, wrong role, reversed arrow or incomplete negation. Then retry that item. Your targeted Group 1 and Group 2 work gives you more practice with individual expressions; this set checks whether the steps hold together.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n    <section id=\\"reference-boundary\\" className=\\"cr-boundary co-section\\" data-reference-boundary data-toc data-toc-title=\\"Permission and instructions\\">\\r\\n      <p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Before the attached practice</p><h2>Keep what the sentence actually gives you</h2>\\r\\n      <p>The core process is now in place. One more distinction matters for the practice: a conditional can give permission or an instruction. Preserve that strength instead of turning it into a report of what someone did.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>Every guest with a ticket may use the cloakroom.</blockquote><p>May use gives permission here. A ticketed guest is allowed to use the cloakroom. The guest could keep their coat on and never go inside.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Lena is a guest with a ticket. Do we know that she used the cloakroom?\\" options={[\'Yes, the ticket establishes that she used it.\', \'No, we know she is allowed to use it.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You turned permission into a completed action. Lena meets the condition for permission, but we have no fact reporting her use of the cloakroom.\', \'The rule gives Lena permission because she is a guest with a ticket. Whether she used the cloakroom remains open.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Now compare an instruction. “If your coat is wet, hang it on the rack” tells you what to do with a wet coat. It does not report that someone followed the instruction.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"The notice says: If a tray is empty, return it to the counter. This tray is empty. What does the instruction establish?\\" options={[\'The tray has been returned.\', \'The instruction calls for returning the tray.\', \'The tray cannot be returned.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You turned an instruction into a completed action. The notice has not reported that anyone returned this tray.\', \'The empty tray meets the condition in the instruction, so the notice calls for returning it. Whether someone returned it is a separate question.\', \'The instruction asks for a return. It does not say a return is impossible.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Allowed to use isn\'t used. Told to return isn\'t returned. Your conclusion should keep the meaning the sentence actually gave you.</p>\\r\\n    </section>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function ConditionalReferenceSequence() { return conditionalReferenceContent(); }\\r\\n\\r\\n// A long stage can contain several reading pages without changing the source order.\\r\\nexport function conditionalReferencePages(): LessonPage[] {\\r\\n  return Children.toArray(conditionalReferenceContent().props.children).flatMap(node => {\\r\\n    if (!isValidElement<{number?: number; title?: string; children?: ReactNode; \'data-toc-title\'?: string}>(node)) return [];\\r\\n    if (node.props.number === 8) {\\r\\n      const parts = Children.toArray(node.props.children);\\r\\n      const sentenceMove = parts.findIndex(child => isValidElement<{children?: ReactNode}>(child) && child.type === \'h3\' && child.props.children === \'The sentence can move. The roles stay the same.\');\\r\\n      const targetMove = parts.findIndex(child => isValidElement<{children?: ReactNode}>(child) && child.type === \'h3\' && child.props.children === \'The indicator can also follow its target\');\\r\\n      return [\\r\\n        { title: \'Build the first diagram: the two sides\', content: <ReferenceStep number={8} label=\\"Build the first diagram · 1 of 3\\" title=\\"Give the relationship a shorter form\\">{parts.slice(0, sentenceMove)}</ReferenceStep> },\\r\\n        { title: \'Build the first diagram: sentence order\', content: <ReferenceStep number={8} part={2} label=\\"Build the first diagram · 2 of 3\\" title=\\"The sentence can move. The roles stay the same.\\">{parts.slice(sentenceMove + 1, targetMove)}</ReferenceStep> },\\r\\n        { title: \'Build the first diagram: target and readback\', content: <ReferenceStep number={8} part={3} label=\\"Build the first diagram · 3 of 3\\" title=\\"Find the target wherever it appears\\">{parts.slice(targetMove + 1)}</ReferenceStep> },\\r\\n        { title: \'Recall enough and required\', content: <section className=\\"co-section\\"><h2>Recall enough and required</h2><p>Put the worked examples aside. Read each new rule, say your answer, and then turn the card to compare your reasoning.</p><StudyDeck group=\\"foundation\\"/></section> },\\r\\n      ];\\r\\n    }\\r\\n    if (node.props.number === 9) {\\r\\n      return [\\r\\n        { title: node.props.title!, content: node },\\r\\n        { title: \'Two possible rooms, one limit\', content: <section className=\\"co-section\\"><h2>Two possible rooms, one limit</h2><NegativeCaseLab/></section> },\\r\\n      ];\\r\\n    }\\r\\n    if (node.props.number === 13) {\\r\\n      return referencePracticeItems.map((item, index) => ({\\r\\n        title: `Use the whole process: sentence ${index + 1}`,\\r\\n        content: <ReferenceStep number={13} part={index > 0 ? index + 1 : undefined} label={`Use the whole process · ${index + 1} of ${referencePracticeItems.length}`} title=\\"Build an original and its contrapositive\\">\\r\\n          {index === 0 && <p>For each new sentence, identify the indicator and both complete conditions. Choose the original diagram, then its contrapositive. Read both back before checking. You can return to any earlier page.</p>}\\r\\n          <IntegratedItem item={item} number={index + 1}/>\\r\\n          <p>If an answer needs revision, name the exact issue: lost qualification, wrong role, reversed arrow or incomplete negation. Then retry that item. Your targeted Group 1 and Group 2 work gives you more practice with individual expressions; this set checks whether the steps hold together.</p>\\r\\n        </ReferenceStep>,\\r\\n      }));\\r\\n    }\\r\\n    return [{ title: node.props.title || node.props[\'data-toc-title\'] || \'Keep the meaning\', content: node }];\\r\\n  });\\r\\n}\\r\\n"}'}]
